## GHSA-jrc7-96c5-q579 · maplibre-gl

2026-09-08 · JavaScript · **CRITICAL** 10.0 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→XSS`

### Root cause

The vulnerability existed because the `DOM.removeAttributes` method iterated directly over `elem.attributes`, which is a live `NamedNodeMap`. When a dangerous attribute was removed using `elem.removeAttribute(name)`, it modified the live collection, causing the loop to skip the next attribute in the original sequence, thus failing to sanitize all malicious attributes.

### Impact

An attacker could bypass the HTML sanitizer, allowing them to inject malicious scripts or content into the DOM. This could lead to arbitrary code execution in the user's browser, session hijacking, or defacement of the web application.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/util/dom.ts
+++ b/src/util/dom.ts
@@ -131,7 +131,7 @@ export class DOM {
 	 * @param elem - The element
 	 */
     private static removeAttributes(elem: Element) {
-        for (const {name, value} of elem.attributes) {
+        for (const {name, value} of Array.from(elem.attributes)) {
             if (!DOM.isPossiblyDangerous(name, value)) continue;
             elem.removeAttribute(name);
         }
```

### Fix summary

The patch fixes the vulnerability by converting the live `NamedNodeMap` returned by `elem.attributes` into a static array using `Array.from()`. This ensures that all attributes are processed and sanitized, even when attributes are removed during the iteration, preventing the sanitizer bypass.

## GHSA-xp7j-h7jc-4w8p · github.com/semaphoreui/semaphore

2026-09-08 · Go · **CRITICAL** 9.9 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→COMMAND`

### Root cause

The application directly passed user-controlled Git URLs to the `git` command-line utility without proper sanitization or argument separation. An attacker could craft a Git URL starting with a hyphen ('-'), which `git` would interpret as a command-line option rather than a repository path, leading to arbitrary command execution.

### Impact

An attacker could execute arbitrary commands on the server where Semaphore U is running, potentially leading to full system compromise, data exfiltration, or denial of service.

### Key diff

```
--- a/db_lib/CmdGitClient.go
+++ b/db_lib/CmdGitClient.go
@@ -115,6 +115,7 @@ func (c CmdGitClient) Clone(r GitRepository) error {
 		"--recursive",
 		"--branch",
 		r.Repository.GitBranch,
+		"--end-of-options",
 		r.Repository.GetGitURL(false),
 		dirName)
 }
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a `ValidateGitURL` function that rejects Git URLs starting with a hyphen. This validation is applied when a repository is created or updated. Additionally, the `--end-of-options` argument is added to all `git` commands that take a user-controlled URL, explicitly telling `git` to treat subsequent arguments as positional parameters rather than options.

## GHSA-92f5-vc22-8j33 · Microsoft.Native.Quic.MsQuic.Schannel

2026-09-08 · C# · **CRITICAL** 9.8 · Pattern: `UNCLASSIFIED`

### Root cause

The vulnerability existed because the QUIC implementation did not properly validate the state of a network path when processing incoming packets. An attacker could send specially crafted packets that would cause the system to attempt to use an inactive or invalid path, leading to memory corruption.

### Impact

An attacker could achieve remote code execution on the target system by exploiting the memory corruption, allowing them to execute arbitrary code with the privileges of the QUIC process.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/core/connection.c
+++ b/src/core/connection.c
@@ -5458,7 +5458,7 @@ QuicConnRecvPostProcessing(
 
     if (Packet->HasNonProbingFrame &&
         Packet->NewLargestPacketNumber &&
-        !(*Path)->IsActive) {
+        !(*Path)->IsActive && (*Path)->InUse) {
```

### Fix summary

The patch adds an additional check to ensure that a network path is not only inactive but also 'InUse' before proceeding with path switching logic. This prevents the system from attempting to use a path that is not properly initialized or valid, thereby mitigating the memory corruption vulnerability.

## GHSA-w6f5-v2h6-g786 · predis/predis

2026-09-08 · PHP · **CRITICAL** 9.8 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→COMMAND`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from how Predis handled pipelined commands, particularly in aggregate connections (like Redis Cluster). It would concatenate all serialized commands into a single buffer and then write this buffer to the connection. This batching, combined with the lack of proper CRLF (carriage return and line feed) sanitization, allowed an attacker to inject arbitrary Redis commands by smuggling CRLF sequences within a command argument, effectively terminating the current command and starting a new one.

### Impact

An attacker could inject arbitrary Redis commands, leading to data manipulation, unauthorized access, or even remote code execution if the Redis server is configured to load modules or execute Lua scripts. Additionally, by injecting malformed commands or a large number of commands, an attacker could trigger a denial of service condition on the Redis server.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/Pipeline/ConnectionErrorProof.php
+++ b/src/Pipeline/ConnectionErrorProof.php
@@ -92,14 +92,12 @@ protected function executeCluster(ClusterInterface $connection, SplQueue $comman
         $responses = [];
         $sizeOfPipe = count($commands);
         $exceptions = [];
-        $buffer = '';
 
         foreach ($commands as $command) {
-            $buffer .= $command->serializeCommand();
+            $nodeConnection = $connection->getConnectionByCommand($command);
+            $nodeConnection->write($command->serializeCommand());
         }
 
-        $connection->write($buffer);
-
         for ($i = 0; $i < $sizeOfPipe; ++$i) {
```

### Fix summary

The patch refactors the command writing logic for pipelined commands. Instead of buffering all commands and writing them in one go, it now iterates through each command and writes it individually to the appropriate node connection, especially for aggregate connections. This prevents CRLF smuggling by ensuring each command is sent as a distinct unit, rather than being part of a larger, potentially injectable buffer.

## GHSA-p293-qw3h-jr36 · next

2026-09-08 · JavaScript · **CRITICAL** 9.0 · Pattern: `PATH_TRAVERSAL→FILE_WRITE`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from a path traversal flaw in how Next.js handled cache file paths, particularly on Windows. The `getFilePath` function did not adequately sanitize user-controlled input (`pathname`), allowing attackers to use backslashes (`\`) to traverse directories. This was compounded by an incomplete escaping mechanism that did not account for backslashes, enabling an attacker to write arbitrary files outside the intended cache directories.

### Impact

An unauthenticated attacker could achieve remote code execution by writing malicious files (e.g., `.js` files) to sensitive locations on the server, which would then be executed by the Node.js process.

### Key diff

```
--- a/packages/next/src/server/lib/incremental-cache/file-system-cache.ts
+++ b/packages/next/src/server/lib/incremental-cache/file-system-cache.ts
@@ -456,26 +456,31 @@ export default class FileSystemCache implements CacheHandler {
     await writer.wait()
   }
 
-  private getFilePath(pathname: string, kind: IncrementalCacheKind): string {
+  private getFilePath(key: string, kind: IncrementalCacheKind): string {
+    let rootDir: string
     switch (kind) {
       case IncrementalCacheKind.FETCH:
         // we store in .next/cache/fetch-cache so it can be persisted
         // across deploys
-        return path.join(
-          this.serverDistDir,
-          '..',
-          'cache',
-          'fetch-cache',
-          pathname
-        )
+        rootDir = path.join(this.serverDistDir, '..', 'cache', 'fetch-cache')
+        break
       case IncrementalCacheKind.PAGES:
-        return path.join(this.serverDistDir, 'pages', pathname)
+        rootDir = path.join(this.serverDistDir, 'pages')
+        break
       case IncrementalCacheKind.IMAGE:
       case IncrementalCacheKind.APP_PAGE:
       case IncrementalCacheKind.APP_ROUTE:
-        return path.join(this.serverDistDir, 'app', pathname)
+        rootDir = path.join(this.serverDistDir, 'app')
+        break
       default:
         throw new Error(`Unexpected file path kind: ${kind}`)
     }
+    const filePath = path.join(rootDir, key)
+    if (!(filePath.startsWith(rootDir + path.sep) || filePath === rootDir)) {
+      throw new Error(`Invalid file path: ${filePath}`)
+    }
+
+    return filePath
   }
 }
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a strict path validation check in `getFilePath` to ensure that the final file path remains within the intended root directory. Additionally, the `escapePathDelimiters` function was updated to include backslashes (`\`) in the set of characters that need to be escaped, preventing path traversal on Windows systems.

---

## GHSA-2x7j-588g-ccc2 · nodemailer

2026-09-08 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from the `addressparser` library, which Nodemailer uses. When processing a list of email addresses, the deduplication logic had a quadratic time complexity (O(n²)). This meant that as the number of addresses in a crafted list increased, the processing time grew exponentially, leading to excessive resource consumption.

### Impact

An attacker could send an email with a specially crafted, long list of recipients (e.g., in the To, Cc, or Bcc fields) to a server using Nodemailer. This would cause the server to spend an inordinate amount of time processing the addresses, leading to a denial of service for legitimate users or potentially crashing the application.

### Key diff

```
--- a/lib/mime-node/index.js
+++ b/lib/mime-node/index.js
@@ -904,9 +904,10 @@ class MimeNode {
                 this._envelope.from = list[0].address;
             }
         }
+        const seenRecipients = new Set();
         ['to', 'cc', 'bcc'].forEach(key => {
             if (envelope[key]) {
-                this._convertAddresses(this._parseEnvelopeAddresses(envelope[key]), this._envelope.to);
+                this._convertAddresses(this._parseEnvelopeAddresses(envelope[key]), this._envelope.to, seenRecipients);
             }
         });
```

### Fix summary

The patch addresses the quadratic time complexity by introducing `Set` objects to efficiently track seen recipients. Instead of repeatedly iterating through existing lists for deduplication, a `Set` provides O(1) average time complexity for lookups, thereby reducing the overall processing time for address lists from O(n²) to O(n).

## GHSA-f833-7jw8-xwrv · nltk

2026-09-08 · Python · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `PATH_TRAVERSAL→FILE_READ`

### Root cause

The previous fix for CVE-2026-54292 only prevented path traversal sequences (e.g., '..') in filenames. It did not account for symbolic links (symlinks) planted within the corpus directory itself, which could point outside the intended root, allowing an attacker to bypass the existing sandbox by tricking the application into resolving a symlink to an arbitrary file.

### Impact

An attacker could read arbitrary files on the system where the NLTK application is running, by crafting a malicious corpus that includes symlinks pointing outside the intended corpus root.

### Key diff

```
--- a/nltk/corpus/reader/framenet.py
+++ b/nltk/corpus/reader/framenet.py
@@ -779,6 +779,26 @@ def _reject_unsafe_path_component(value, kind):
         raise FramenetError(f"Invalid {kind}: {value!r}")
 
 
+def _validate_in_root(locpath, root, context):
+    """Reject a resolved path that escapes the corpus root through a symlink.
+    ...
+    """
+    from nltk.pathsec import validate_path
+
+    validate_path(locpath, context=context, required_root=root)
+
 
 class AttrDict(dict):
     """A class that wraps a dict and allows accessing the keys of the
@@ -1405,6 +1425,7 @@ def doc(self, fn_docid):
         _reject_unsafe_path_component(xmlfname, "document filename")
 
         locpath = self.abspath(os.path.join(self._fulltext_dir, xmlfname))
+        _validate_in_root(locpath, self.root, "FramenetCorpusReader")
 
         with XMLCorpusView(locpath, "fullTextAnnotation") as view:
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new validation function, `_validate_in_root`, which uses `nltk.pathsec.validate_path` to ensure that all resolved paths, including those involving symlinks, remain strictly within the defined corpus root. This function is called at critical points where file paths are constructed and accessed, such as when retrieving document, frame, or LU files.

## GHSA-qfvm-cv95-jqjf · multer

2026-09-08 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 7.5 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from a file descriptor leak. When an upload was aborted, the write stream to the temporary file was not properly closed or destroyed. This led to file descriptors remaining open, eventually exhausting the system's available file descriptors and causing a Denial of Service.

### Impact

An attacker could exhaust the server's file descriptor limit by repeatedly initiating and then aborting file uploads, making the server unable to open new files or connections, thus leading to a Denial of Service.

### Key diff

```
--- a/storage/disk.js
+++ b/storage/disk.js
@@ -35,15 +41,17 @@ DiskStorage.prototype._handleFile = function _handleFile (req, file, cb) {
 
       var finalPath = path.join(destination, filename)
 
-      if (file.stream.destroyed) return
+      if (file.stream.destroyed) return cb(new MulterError('STREAM_DESTROYED'))
 
       var outStream = fs.createWriteStream(finalPath)
 
       file.path = finalPath
+      openStreams.set(file, outStream)
+      outStream.once('close', function () { openStreams.delete(file) })
+
+      pipeline(file.stream, outStream, function (err) {
+        if (err) return cb(err)
 
-      file.stream.pipe(outStream)
-      outStream.on('error', cb)
-      outStream.on('finish', function () {
         cb(null, {
           destination: destination,
           filename: filename,
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a mechanism to track open write streams and ensures they are properly closed or destroyed when an upload is aborted or an error occurs. It uses `stream.pipeline` for better error handling and stream management, and explicitly destroys the output stream and waits for its closure before unlinking the file.

## GHSA-r6gq-whwq-mvg9 · nltk

2026-09-08 · Python · **HIGH** 6.2 · Pattern: `PATH_TRAVERSAL→FILE_READ`

### Root cause

The NLTK library's CorpusReader allowed paths to be resolved without fully sanitizing or resolving symlinks, especially when dealing with ZIP archives or user-provided paths. This meant that an attacker could craft a path containing symlinks or directory traversal sequences (e.g., `../`) to escape the intended corpus root directory.

### Impact

An attacker could read arbitrary local files outside of the designated NLTK data directories, potentially leading to sensitive information disclosure or further system compromise.

### Key diff

```
--- a/nltk/pathsec.py
+++ b/nltk/pathsec.py
@@ -77,6 +77,10 @@ def validate_path(path_input, context="NLTK"):
             if parsed.scheme == "file":
-                raw = urllib.request.url2pathname(parsed.path)
+                raw = unquote(parsed.path)
 
-        target = Path(raw).resolve()
+        # Resolve path to catch symlink escapes
+        try:
+            target = Path(raw).resolve()
+        except (OSError, ValueError):
```

### Fix summary

The patch enhances path validation by consistently using `Path(raw).resolve()` to canonicalize paths and resolve symlinks, preventing directory traversal. It also introduces a `required_root` parameter to `validate_path` for stricter scoped sandboxing and improves ZIP archive validation to prevent Zip-Slip attacks by resolving member paths relative to the target root.

## GHSA-568f-pv23-39p4 · nltk

2026-09-08 · Python · **HIGH** 5.9 · Pattern: `PATH_TRAVERSAL→FILE_READ`

### Root cause

The NLTK FrameNet and NKJP corpus readers constructed file paths by directly interpolating values (like document filenames, frame names, or lexical unit IDs) from potentially untrusted corpus data into `os.path.join` calls. This allowed specially crafted names containing path traversal sequences (e.g., `../`, `C:`, `\\host\share`) to escape the intended corpus root directory.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious corpus index or data file that, when processed by the NLTK readers, would cause the application to read arbitrary files outside the intended corpus root directory. This could lead to information disclosure of sensitive files on the system.

### Key diff

```
--- a/nltk/corpus/reader/framenet.py
+++ b/nltk/corpus/reader/framenet.py
@@ -1362,8 +1385,15 @@ def doc(self, fn_docid):
         except KeyError as e:  # probably means that fn_docid was not in the index
             raise FramenetError(f"Unknown document id: {fn_docid}") from e
 
+        _reject_unsafe_path_component(xmlfname, "document filename")
+
         # construct the path name for the xml file containing the document info
-        locpath = os.path.join(f"{self._root}", self._fulltext_dir, xmlfname)
+        locpath = self.abspath(os.path.join(self._fulltext_dir, xmlfname))
```

### Fix summary

A new helper function `_reject_unsafe_path_component` was introduced to validate and reject any input value that contains path separators, '..', or Windows drive/UNC qualifiers. This function is called before constructing file paths in `doc()`, `frame_by_name()`, and `_lu_file()`. Additionally, the file path resolution was updated to use `self.abspath()` which leverages NLTK's `PathPointer` and `nltk.pathsec` sandbox for safer file access.

## GHSA-3hhw-38pf-pxj6 · nltk

2026-09-08 · Python · **MODERATE** 5.5 · Pattern: `PATH_TRAVERSAL→FILE_READ`

### Root cause

The `IPIPANCorpusReader` class in NLTK performed path manipulations (e.g., `.replace()`) on `PathPointer` objects, which caused them to be silently converted into plain strings. This conversion stripped away the security checks normally applied by `nltk.pathsec`, allowing `open()` calls to bypass path validation. Consequently, a symlink placed within the corpus root could point to arbitrary files outside the intended directory.

### Impact

An attacker could read any file on the filesystem that the NLTK process has permissions to access, by crafting a malicious symlink within a controlled corpus directory.

### Key diff

```
--- a/nltk/corpus/reader/ipipan.py
+++ b/nltk/corpus/reader/ipipan.py
@@ -187,6 +187,24 @@ def _list_morph_files_by(self, tag, values, map=None):
         return list(ret_fileids)
 
     def _get_tag(self, f, tag):
+        from nltk.pathsec import validate_path
+
+        validate_path(f, context="IPIPANCorpusReader", required_root=self.root)
+
         tags = []
         with open(f) as infile:
             header = infile.read()
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces an explicit call to `nltk.pathsec.validate_path()` before opening files in the `_get_tag` method. This ensures that the resolved path is validated against the corpus root, preventing symlink-based path traversal even if the `PathPointer` object has been implicitly converted to a string.

## GHSA-5wp5-5229-5g6q · nltk

2026-09-08 · Python · **MODERATE** 5.3 · Pattern: `MISSING_VERIFICATION→SIGNATURE`

### Root cause

The NLTK downloader used MD5 for checksum verification of downloaded packages. MD5 is cryptographically weak and susceptible to collision attacks, meaning an attacker could craft a malicious package with the same MD5 hash as a legitimate one.

### Impact

An attacker could inject a malicious package into the NLTK download process, leading to arbitrary code execution on the user's system when the package is installed or used.

### Key diff

```
--- a/nltk/downloader.py
+++ b/nltk/downloader.py
-        if md5_hexdigest(filepath) != info.checksum:
+        if sha256_hexdigest(filepath) != info.sha256_checksum:
```

### Fix summary

The patch updates the NLTK downloader to use SHA256 for checksum verification instead of MD5. This significantly strengthens the integrity check, making it much harder for an attacker to forge a malicious package with a matching hash.

---

## GHSA-2xp9-vwfh-vxw4 · next

2026-09-08 · JavaScript · **CRITICAL** 0.0 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→COMMAND`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from the Next.js Image Optimization API's handling of AVIF files. Specifically, the `sharp` library, used for image processing, was configured to block certain operations but failed to explicitly block `VipsForeignLoadHeif` (which handles AVIF). This oversight, combined with the ability to provide external image URLs, allowed an attacker to craft a malicious AVIF file that, when processed, could lead to arbitrary command execution.

### Impact

An unauthenticated attacker could achieve remote code execution on the server running the Next.js application, potentially leading to full system compromise, data exfiltration, or denial of service.

### Key diff

```
--- a/packages/next/src/server/image-optimizer.ts
+++ b/packages/next/src/server/image-optimizer.ts
@@ -95,7 +95,6 @@ export function getSharp(
     _sharp.block({ operation: ['VipsForeignLoad'] })
     _sharp.unblock({
       operation: [
-        'VipsForeignLoadHeif', // avif
         'VipsForeignLoadJpeg',
         'VipsForeignLoadNsgif',
         'VipsForeignLoadPng',
```

### Fix summary

The patch addresses the vulnerability by explicitly adding AVIF to the list of bypassed image types for optimization, preventing `sharp` from processing them. It also removes AVIF from the list of valid extensions for blur data URL generation, effectively disabling AVIF optimization and blur generation to mitigate the risk of malicious file processing.

## GHSA-3gq4-3j92-5w49 · nltk

2026-09-08 · Python · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `PATH_TRAVERSAL→FILE_READ`

### Root cause

The NLTK CorpusReader and related functions did not sufficiently validate user-supplied 'root' paths against the NLTK data sandbox. This allowed an attacker to specify paths outside the intended NLTK data directories, leading to arbitrary file access or execution of SQLite databases from unauthorized locations.

### Impact

An attacker could read arbitrary files on the system, potentially including sensitive data, or execute malicious SQLite databases if they could control the 'root' parameter passed to CorpusReader or PanLexLiteCorpusReader.

### Key diff

```
--- a/nltk/corpus/reader/api.py
+++ b/nltk/corpus/reader/api.py
@@ -70,6 +71,18 @@ def __init__(self, root, fileids, encoding="utf8", tagset=None):
               for normalizing or converting the POS tags returned by the
               ``tagged_...()`` methods.
         """
+        if pathsec.ENFORCE and isinstance(root, str):
+            pathsec.validate_path(root, context="CorpusReader.__init__")
 
         # Convert the root to a path pointer, if necessary.
         if isinstance(root, str) and not isinstance(root, PathPointer):

--- a/nltk/corpus/reader/panlex_lite.py
+++ b/nltk/corpus/reader/panlex_lite.py
@@ -43,7 +43,16 @@ class PanLexLiteCorpusReader(CorpusReader):
     """
 
     def __init__(self, root):
-        self._c = sqlite3.connect(os.path.join(root, "db.sqlite")).cursor()
+        from nltk.pathsec import validate_path
+
+        db_path = os.path.join(root, "db.sqlite")
+        validate_path(db_path, context="PanLexLiteCorpusReader", required_root=root)
+        self._c = sqlite3.connect(db_path).cursor()
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces `nltk.pathsec.validate_path` calls at critical points in `CorpusReader.__init__`, `find_corpus_fileids`, and `PanLexLiteCorpusReader.__init__`. These calls ensure that all provided paths, especially the 'root' parameter, are validated against the NLTK data sandbox before being used, preventing access to unauthorized file system locations.

## GHSA-6ww7-3frv-cqxh · nltk

2026-09-08 · Python · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `SSRF→INTERNAL_ACCESS`

### Root cause

The existing SSRF protection in NLTK relied on pinning the validated destination IP. However, when a proxy was configured, NLTK's validation only reflected its local view of the host, not the actual destination the proxy would reach. This allowed an attacker to bypass the protection using DNS rebinding or by leveraging a proxy with an internal network view.

### Impact

An attacker could bypass NLTK's SSRF protection to make the application send requests to arbitrary internal or external systems, potentially leading to information disclosure, unauthorized actions, or access to sensitive internal resources.

### Key diff

```
--- a/nltk/pathsec.py
+++ b/nltk/pathsec.py
@@ -519,6 +562,14 @@ def urlopen(url, *args, **kwargs):
             handlers.append(urllib.request.ProxyHandler({}))
         handlers.append(_SafeHTTPHandler())
         handlers.append(_SafeHTTPSHandler())
+    else:
+        # Proxied: the proxy resolves the name and performs the egress, so the
+        # earlier validate_network_url() only reflects NLTK's *local* view of the
+        # host, not what the proxy actually reaches. A rebinding name or a proxy
+        # with an internal network view re-opens SSRF (CWE-918, GHSA-6ww7). We
+        # cannot pin through a proxy, so fail closed unless the operator has
+        # asserted the proxy is trusted.
+        _reject_unpinnable_proxied_fetch(url_str)
 
     opener = urllib.request.build_opener(*handlers)
     return opener.open(url, *args, **kwargs)
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new mechanism to explicitly disallow proxied fetches by default when SSRF protection is enforced. It adds a new configuration option, `ALLOW_PROXIED_FETCH`, and an environment variable, `NLTK_ALLOW_PROXIED_URLOPEN`, which must be explicitly set to true by the operator to permit proxied requests. If not opted in, proxied fetches are now rejected with a `PermissionError`.

## GHSA-96p9-rh4f-92cf · winml-cli

2026-09-08 · Python · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `CORS_MISCONFIGURATION→ORIGIN`

### Root cause

The application previously used a highly permissive CORS configuration (allow_origins=['*'], allow_methods=['*'], allow_headers=['*']) for its local development server. This allowed any origin to make cross-origin requests, including to sensitive endpoints that should only be accessible from the local machine or same origin.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious web page that, when visited by a user, could make cross-origin requests to the local WinML server. This could lead to remote code execution (RCE) by interacting with sensitive endpoints like `/v1/cli` or `/v1/models` which are intended for local client interaction only.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/winml/modelkit/serve/app.py
+++ b/src/winml/modelkit/serve/app.py
@@ -33,14 +33,14 @@
 from pathlib import Path
 from typing import TYPE_CHECKING, Any, cast
 
-from fastapi import FastAPI, File, Form, HTTPException, UploadFile
-from fastapi.middleware.cors import CORSMiddleware
+from fastapi import FastAPI, File, Form, HTTPException, Request, UploadFile
 from fastapi.responses import FileResponse
 from fastapi.staticfiles import StaticFiles
 
 from .. import __version__
 from ..inference import InferenceEngine
 from ..inference.types import PredictionResult
+from ._security import SameOriginMiddleware, _is_loopback_client
 from .cli_api import CliRequest, CliResponse, _run_with_semaphore
 from .manager import ModelSlotManager, SingleModelManager
 from .schema import (
@@ -213,13 +213,13 @@ async def lifespan(app: FastAPI) -> AsyncIterator[None]:
         ),
         lifespan=lifespan,
     )
-    # Permissive CORS for local dev server; no credentials to protect.
     app.add_middleware(
-        CORSMiddleware,
-        allow_origins=["*"],
-        allow_credentials=False,
-        allow_methods=["*"],
-        allow_headers=["*"],
+        SameOriginMiddleware,
+        loopback_only_routes={
+            "/v1/cli": None,
+            "/v1/ep": {"POST"},
+            "/v1/models": {"POST", "DELETE"},
+        },
     )
 
     # Serve demo UI at /demo
```

### Fix summary

The patch removes the overly permissive `CORSMiddleware` and introduces a custom `SameOriginMiddleware`. This new middleware enforces strict same-origin policies for most routes and specifically restricts certain sensitive routes (e.g., `/v1/cli`, `/v1/ep`, `/v1/models`) to loopback clients only, preventing cross-origin and remote access to these functionalities.

## GHSA-97qj-x29f-37w7 · nltk

2026-09-08 · Python · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The NLTK library used `xml.etree.ElementTree` for XML parsing, which by default honors `<!ENTITY>` declarations in a document's internal DTD subset. This allows a small XML file to define entities that recursively expand, leading to an arbitrarily large memory consumption, a classic 'billion laughs' attack.

### Impact

An attacker could provide a specially crafted XML file that, when parsed by NLTK, would consume excessive memory, leading to a denial of service by crashing the application or exhausting system resources.

### Key diff

```
--- a/nltk/downloader.py
+++ b/nltk/downloader.py
-from defusedxml.ElementTree import parse as safe_parse
+from nltk.xmlsec import parse as safe_parse
...
-            xml = ElementTree.parse(xml)
+            xml = safe_parse(xml)
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new module `nltk.xmlsec` which provides safer XML parsing functions (`fromstring` and `parse`). These functions either use `defusedxml` if available or implement a fallback mechanism that pre-scans XML documents using `xml.parsers.expat` to explicitly forbid entity declarations and external entity references before passing them to `ElementTree` for parsing. All internal NLTK XML parsing points are updated to use these new safe functions.

## GHSA-c4c3-7fpv-j4q5 · io.netty:netty-handler

2026-09-08 · Java · **CRITICAL** 0.0 · Pattern: `UNCLASSIFIED`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from insufficient validation of input lengths and characters in various protocols (SOCKS4, SOCKS5, MQTT). Specifically, SOCKS5 fields were not checked for length before writing, potentially leading to truncation or malformed packets. SOCKS4 fields did not sanitize for NUL bytes, which are used as delimiters, allowing an attacker to inject NUL bytes to prematurely terminate fields. MQTT fields like clientIdentifier, willTopic, and userName also lacked proper validation, enabling similar issues.

### Impact

An attacker could craft malformed packets to bypass security checks, cause protocol parsing errors, or potentially lead to denial of service. In the context of SNI routing, this could cause a TLS ClientHello to be fragmented in a way that bypasses SNI-based routing, forcing a fallback to a default SslContext, which might have different security policies or expose unintended services.

### Key diff

```
--- a/codec-socks/src/main/java/io/netty/handler/codec/socksx/v5/Socks5ClientEncoder.java
+++ b/codec-socks/src/main/java/io/netty/handler/codec/socksx/v5/Socks5ClientEncoder.java
@@ -80,7 +80,7 @@ private static void encodeAuthMethodRequest(Socks5InitialRequest msg, ByteBuf ou
 
         final List<Socks5AuthMethod> authMethods = msg.authMethods();
         final int numAuthMethods = authMethods.size();
-        out.writeByte(numAuthMethods);
+        writeFieldLength(out, numAuthMethods);
 
         if (authMethods instanceof RandomAccess) {
             for (int i = 0; i < numAuthMethods; i ++) {
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces explicit length checks for SOCKS5 fields, throwing an EncoderException if the length exceeds 255 or is negative. For SOCKS4, it adds a `sanitize` method to check for and reject NUL bytes in string fields. For MQTT, it enhances validation for `clientIdentifier`, `willTopic`, and `userName` fields by calling new or updated `isValid` methods, ensuring they conform to protocol specifications.

## GHSA-fxf7-vhh8-7vpq · cakephp/database

2026-09-08 · PHP · **CRITICAL** 0.0 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→SQL`

### Root cause

The vulnerability existed because the `jsonValue()` function in CakePHP's `FunctionsBuilder`, when used with the `PostgresDriver`, was not properly quoting or sanitizing the second argument (the JSON path) before embedding it into a SQL query. This allowed an attacker to inject arbitrary SQL into the `JSONB_PATH_QUERY` function.

### Impact

An attacker could inject malicious SQL into database queries, potentially leading to unauthorized data access, modification, or deletion, and could even execute arbitrary commands on the database server depending on the database configuration and privileges.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/Database/Driver/Postgres.php
+++ b/src/Database/Driver/Postgres.php
@@ -328,9 +328,7 @@ protected function _transformFunctionExpression(FunctionExpression $expression):
                 $expression->setName('JSONB_PATH_QUERY')
                     ->iterateParts(function ($p, $key) {
                         if ($key === 0) {
-                            $p = sprintf('%s::jsonb', $p);
-                        } elseif ($key === 1) {
-                            $p = sprintf("'%s'::jsonpath", $this->quoteIdentifier($p['value']));
+                            return sprintf('%s::jsonb', $p);
                         }
 
                         return $p;
```

### Fix summary

The patch removes the explicit quoting of the second argument (the JSON path) in the `_transformFunctionExpression` method for the `Postgres` driver. Instead of manually quoting with `sprintf("'%s'::jsonpath", $this->quoteIdentifier($p['value']))`, the system now relies on the underlying database abstraction layer to handle the parameter binding and proper escaping, preventing direct SQL injection.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 32 occurrences**
> Languages: Go, PHP, JavaScript, Ruby, Java, C#, Erlang, Python

## GHSA-p4rw-rvv2-7xwr · nltk

2026-09-08 · Python · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `RACE_CONDITION→TOCTOU`

### Root cause

The NLTK corpus readers, specifically `CrubadanCorpusReader`, used `builtins.open` to access files. While a path validation mechanism (`validate_path`) existed, it was susceptible to Time-of-Check-to-Time-of-Use (TOCTOU) race conditions and hardlink escapes. An attacker could swap a symlink or create a hardlink after the path validation but before the file was actually opened, leading to access outside the intended root directory.

### Impact

An attacker could read arbitrary files on the system by crafting malicious symlinks or hardlinks within the trusted corpus root, bypassing the existing path security checks. This could lead to sensitive information disclosure.

### Key diff

```
--- a/nltk/pathsec.py
+++ b/nltk/pathsec.py
@@ -599,4 +687,14 @@ def open(file, mode="r", *, context="pathsec.open", required_root=None, **kwargs
 
     # 4. Execution Substitution: validate and open the pure primitive, discarding the original object
     validate_path(raw_path, context=context, required_root=required_root)
+    # 5. For read modes under enforcement on POSIX, additionally close the
+    # validate-then-open symlink TOCTOU and the hardlink escape that a path-based
+    # check cannot see. Write/append/update flows and non-POSIX keep the plain
+    # open (their containment is unchanged from before).
+    if ENFORCE and os.name == "posix" and _is_readonly_mode(mode):
+        # The hardened path owns this open entirely: it raises PermissionError /
+        # ValueError on a security violation and the usual OSError (e.g.
+        # FileNotFoundError) on a genuine open failure. We must NOT fall back to
+        # builtins.open on OSError -- retrying the raw path would follow a symlink
+        # the hardened open deliberately refused, reopening the TOCTOU leak.
+        return _hardened_read_open(raw_path, mode, context, required_root, **kwargs)
     return builtins.open(raw_path, mode=mode, **kwargs)
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new hardened file opening function, `_hardened_read_open`, which uses `os.open` with `O_NOFOLLOW` to prevent symlink following at open time. It also checks for hardlinks (`st_nlink > 1`) and re-validates the *actual* opened file descriptor's real path from the kernel, closing TOCTOU windows and hardlink escapes. The `CrubadanCorpusReader` was updated to use this new hardened open function.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 18 occurrences**
> Languages: JavaScript, Go, Python, RUST
> → GHSA-gj9q-8w99-mp8j
> → GHSA-rmx9-2pp3-xhcr
> → GHSA-5h3g-6xhh-rg6p
> → GHSA-h3ww-q6xx-w7x3
> → GHSA-r95r-rj6r-c39x

> → GHSA-4vgr-h27g-cf9p
> → GHSA-239g-2685-54x3

## GHSA-rhp5-r9x4-f5g2 · nltk

2026-09-08 · Python · **CRITICAL** 0.0 · Pattern: `DESERIALIZATION→RCE`

### Root cause

The NLTK library previously used `pickle_load` without sufficient restrictions when loading trained models (TransitionParser) and Punkt tokenizer data. This allowed an attacker to craft malicious pickle files that, when deserialized, could execute arbitrary code due to the inherent insecurity of Python's `pickle` module when processing untrusted input.

### Impact

An attacker could achieve arbitrary code execution on the system running the NLTK application by providing a specially crafted malicious pickle file. This could lead to full system compromise, data exfiltration, or denial of service.

### Key diff

```
--- a/nltk/parse/transitionparser.py
+++ b/nltk/parse/transitionparser.py
-from nltk.picklesec import pickle_load
+from nltk.picklesec import allowlisted_pickle_load
...
         with open(modelFile, "rb") as f:
-            model = pickle_load(f)
+            model = allowlisted_pickle_load(f, allowed_modules=_MODEL_ALLOWED_MODULES)
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces `AllowlistUnpickler` and `allowlisted_pickle_load` functions, which restrict the modules and globals that can be reconstructed during deserialization. The `TransitionParser` and Punkt tokenizer now use these new functions with specific allowlists (e.g., 'numpy', 'scipy', 'sklearn' for models, and 'nltk.tokenize.punkt', 'nltk.tokenize' for Punkt data) to prevent the deserialization of arbitrary code gadgets.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 25 occurrences**
> Languages: Java, Python, PHP, JavaScript, Ruby, Erlang

> → GHSA-69w3-r845-3855

## GHSA-x5ph-mj9p-rfr8 · nltk

2026-09-08 · Python · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `PATH_TRAVERSAL→FILE_READ`

### Root cause

The `StreamBackedCorpusView` class in NLTK's corpus reader utility did not properly enforce path security when opening files. It directly used Python's built-in `open()` function with user-controlled file paths, bypassing NLTK's intended secure file opening mechanism (`nltk.pathsec.open`). Additionally, the `find_corpus_fileids` function, when traversing directories, did not adequately restrict directory traversal through symlinks, allowing an attacker to read files outside the intended corpus root.

### Impact

An attacker could supply a specially crafted file path or leverage symlinks within a corpus to read arbitrary local files on the system where the NLTK application is running. This could lead to the disclosure of sensitive information.

### Key diff

```
--- a/nltk/corpus/reader/util.py
+++ b/nltk/corpus/reader/util.py
@@ -209,10 +210,10 @@ def _open(self):
             self._stream = self._fileid.open(self._encoding)
         elif self._encoding:
             self._stream = SeekableUnicodeStreamReader(
-                open(self._fileid, "rb"), self._encoding
+                _secure_open(self._fileid, "rb"), self._encoding
             )
         else:
-            self._stream = open(self._fileid, "rb")
+            self._stream = _secure_open(self._fileid, "rb")
```

### Fix summary

The patch modifies `StreamBackedCorpusView._open` to consistently use `nltk.pathsec.open` instead of the standard `open()` function, ensuring all file access goes through the security checks. It also enhances `find_corpus_fileids` to prevent directory traversal via symlinks by resolving real paths and ensuring all traversed directories remain within the resolved corpus root.

## GHSA-x99w-6fgc-pmfw · nltk

2026-09-08 · Python · **CRITICAL** 0.0 · Pattern: `DESERIALIZATION→RCE`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from an incomplete allowlisting mechanism in the pickle deserialization process. While NLTK attempted to restrict the types of objects that could be unpickled, certain dangerous functions or modules could still be resolved and executed if they resided within an otherwise allowlisted namespace (e.g., numpy, nltk.tokenize) or if a dotted name allowed attribute traversal to reach dangerous functions.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious pickle payload that, when deserialized, would execute arbitrary code on the system where the NLTK library is used, leading to full system compromise.

### Key diff

```
--- a/nltk/picklesec.py
+++ b/nltk/picklesec.py
@@ -110,20 +192,118 @@ def __init__(
         self._allowed_globals = set(allowed_globals)
         self._allowed_modules = tuple(allowed_modules)
 
+    @staticmethod
+    def _module_denied(module: str) -> bool:
+        return any(
+            module == deny or module.startswith(deny + ".")
+            for deny in _DENIED_MODULE_PREFIXES
+        )
+
     def _module_allowed(self, module: str) -> bool:
         return any(
             module == name or module.startswith(name + ".")
             for name in self._allowed_modules
         )
 
+    def _resolve(self, module: str, name: str) -> Any:
+        obj = super().find_class(module, name)
+        if isinstance(obj, types.ModuleType):
+            raise pickle.UnpicklingError(
+                f"global '{module}.{name}' resolves to a module object, which is "
+                "not a reconstructable class/function"
+            )
+        true_module = getattr(obj, "__module__", None)
+        true_qualname = getattr(obj, "__qualname__", name)
+        if (true_module, true_qualname) not in _SAFE_DENIED_GLOBALS:
+            if true_module and self._module_denied(true_module):
+                raise pickle.UnpicklingError(
+                    f"global '{module}.{name}' re-exports {true_module}."
+                    f"{true_qualname} from a denied module"
+                )
+        if (true_module, true_qualname) in _DENIED_GLOBALS:
+            raise pickle.UnpicklingError(
+                f"global '{module}.{name}' ({true_module}.{true_qualname}) is a "
+                "denied callable and cannot be reconstructed from an untrusted "
+                "pickle"
+            )
+        return obj
+
     def find_class(self, module: str, name: str) -> Any:
-        if (module, name) in self._allowed_globals or self._module_allowed(module):
-            return super().find_class(module, name)
+        if "." in name:
+            raise pickle.UnpicklingError(
+                f"global '{module}.{name}' has a dotted name, which is forbidden "
+                "(attribute-traversal pickle RCE, GHSA-4489)"
+            )
+        if name.startswith("__") and name.endswith("__"):
+            raise pickle.UnpicklingError(
+                f"global '{module}.{name}' has a dunder name, which is forbidden"
+            )
+        if (module, name) in _DENIED_GLOBALS:
+            raise pickle.UnpicklingError(
+                f"global '{module}.{name}' is a denied callable and cannot be "
+                "reconstructed from an untrusted pickle"
+            )
+        # Guard 4: an exact allowlisted
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a comprehensive denylist for modules and specific globals, and adds several new guards to the `find_class` method. It explicitly denies dotted names, dunder names, and modules known to contain dangerous functions, even if their parent namespace is allowlisted. It also performs post-resolution checks to ensure the true module and qualname of an object are not from a denied source.
